People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi Dr. Smith, it's really nice to meet you too. Thank you so much for seeing me. 

Um, I guess I'm here because... well, it's probably not a huge deal, but I've been having a little trouble at work lately. I'm in advertising sales, and we have these really long strategy meetings. And I just... I keep getting up during them. 

Like, I know I'm supposed to stay seated with everyone else, but I always find an excuse to pop up, even if it's just to refill my water or, you know, pace over by the printer for a second. It's probably just a silly habit, but I just can't seem to stay in my chair when I'm supposed to, and it's starting to make me a little self-conscious. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining that okay.

Um, well... I guess maybe a little bit? I mean, I was a happy kid and I really loved school, but... yeah, I suppose my mind did wander sometimes.

I hope I'm explaining that okay.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling worried or anxious about a wide range of different things, like your job performance, health, or personal finances, even when there isn't a specific reason to be?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't really think so. I mean, sales can be a little stressful sometimes, but just the normal amount, you know? I definitely don't sit around worrying about my health or finances if there's no reason to. I'm actually a pretty positive person overall! \n\nI hope that's okay to say, I just... I want to make sure I'm answering your questions right. Is that what you meant?\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your childhood and school years, were you frequently in trouble for breaking rules or acting without thinking about the consequences?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, not really. I mean, I was a pretty good kid, I think. I definitely wasn't, like, a troublemaker or anything like that. \n\nI guess sometimes I could be a little... energetic? Like maybe talking out of turn once in a while or just getting caught up in the moment playing with friends. But I always tried really hard to follow the rules and make my teachers happy. I definitely wasn't getting into serious trouble or doing bad things on purpose. \n\nI hope that answers your question? I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right kind of information.\n\nDoctor: When you are working on tasks that aren't particularly exciting, do you often find it difficult to keep your mind on what you are doing without getting distracted?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, I guess I do have a little trouble with that sometimes. It's really not terrible, but... well, like, if I'm in one of those longer strategy meetings, or if I have to read through a really long client brief, I always start out super focused. I really want to do a good job! But then... I don't know, after a few minutes, I'll kind of realize that I've just been staring at the exact same slide, or the same paragraph, and I honestly have no idea what the last point even was. \n\nIt's probably just normal fatigue, right? Like, everyone gets a little bored reading long documents? I always try to catch myself and refocus right away so I don't miss anything important. Does that sound like what you're asking about?\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your time in elementary school, did you often find it difficult to stay focused on your schoolwork or finish your assignments because your mind would wander?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess maybe a little bit? I mean, I was a happy kid and I really loved school, but... yeah, I suppose my mind did wander sometimes. \n\nI actually remember, um, even way back in elementary school, my teachers would always write on my report cards that I was really bright. But they also said I tended to rush through things, or I'd forget to turn in my homework. And my desk was always just... well, it was always stuffed with all these half-finished papers and things. I think I just got really excited about starting new projects and would forget to finish the old ones? And I guess I did talk out of turn sometimes, just because I was so eager to participate. \n\nIt wasn't because I didn't care or anything, I just... I had a lot of energy, I think. Is that normal for kids that age? I hope that's the kind of example you were looking for.\n\nDoctor: Beyond getting up during meetings, do you find that you often feel restless, or feel a need to keep moving even when you're trying to relax?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, I definitely don't feel like I'm constantly pacing around or anything like that. It's definitely not like a strong urge to run a marathon or anything. I'm pretty good at just sitting on the couch when I need to. \n\nI hope that makes sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of how I am at home.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your daily life, do you feel that your struggles with focus and attention cause you more difficulty than your tendency to be restless or act on impulse?\n\nPatient: Um, gosh, I'm not really sure how to answer that? I really try to stay organized so it doesn't become a problem for anyone else. \n\nI hope it's okay that it's been going on for that long? I probably should have mentioned it sooner, I just didn't want to make a big fuss if it was just normal stress from a new role. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your childhood before you were 12 years old, did you experience these same challenges with focusing or feeling the need to move around?\n\nPatient: Um, gosh, well... if I really think back to when I was little, like before high school... Oh, um... well... I guess I wouldn't say *significant* mistakes... but, um, it has come up a little bit at work recently. Um, well... with hobbies, like a fun book or a movie, I think I usually do okay! If it's something I really enjoy, I definitely want to see how it ends. 

But... I guess where I *do* notice that happening—the mind wandering part—is more with work things? Like, um, if I'm reading a long client brief or sitting in one of our longer strategy meetings. I always start out really focused, I promise I do! I have my notebook out and I'm ready to go. But then, after a few minutes, I'll suddenly realize I've just been staring at the exact same slide, or the same paragraph, and I have absolutely no idea what the last point was. 

I always feel so bad when it happens, but I try to just catch myself and reread it really quickly so I don't fall behind or let anyone down. I mean, those corporate documents can be pretty dense and dry, right? So it's probably pretty normal to zone out a little bit compared to when you're watching a fun movie... isn't it?

I've been trying extra hard since then to double-check everything. \n\nI really hope my boss doesn't think I'm a bad employee. I put in a lot of extra hours to make up for it! Does that sound like a normal thing to happen when you're busy, or is that the kind of mistake you're asking about?\n\nDoctor: Aside from your work environment, do you notice these same patterns of getting distracted or feeling the need to move around when you are at home or in other social settings?\n\nPatient: Um, gosh, at home or with friends? I... I don't really think so. Or at least, I haven't really noticed it being an issue. \n\nI mean, I really try to be present when I'm with people. I'd absolutely hate for my friends or family to think I wasn't listening to them, you know? So I make a really big effort to be a good friend and focus on whatever we're doing. Maybe I get up to tidy the apartment a bit when I'm home, or I might shift around a tiny bit during a really long movie, but... that's just normal relaxing, right? It's definitely not like I'm bouncing around or ignoring anyone. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I struggle with those patterns in social settings or at home. I hope that's alright? Is it normal for these things to mostly just happen when you're under a lot of pressure at work?\n\nDoctor: When you are engaged in a hobby or a personal activity that you find relaxing, like reading a book or watching a movie, do you find yourself frequently unable to finish it because your mind wanders or you feel the need to do something else?\n\nPatient: Um, well... with hobbies, like a fun book or a movie, I think I usually do okay! If it's something I really enjoy, I definitely want to see how it ends. \n\nBut... I guess where I *do* notice that happening—the mind wandering part—is more with work things? Like, um, if I'm reading a long client brief or sitting in one of our longer strategy meetings. I always start out really focused, I promise I do! I have my notebook out and I'm ready to go. But then, after a few minutes, I'll suddenly realize I've just been staring at the exact same slide, or the same paragraph, and I have absolutely no idea what the last point was. \n\nI always feel so bad when it happens, but I try to just catch myself and reread it really quickly so I don't fall behind or let anyone down. I mean, those corporate documents can be pretty dense and dry, right? So it's probably pretty normal to zone out a little bit compared to when you're watching a fun movie... isn't it?\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you were a child, did your parents or family members ever mention that you had trouble focusing on tasks or staying still while at home, or was it mostly just something your teachers noticed at school?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 30 / advertising_sales_agent / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"I remember this starting way before high school—my elementary school teachers wrote that I was bright but rushed, talked out of turn, forgot homework, and had a desk stuffed with half-finished papers.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... gosh, thinking back to when I was little... I think it was mostly at school? My parents didn't really say much about it at home. I mean, I was just a happy, energetic kid, you know? 

But, um... I do remember my teachers bringing it up, even way back in elementary school. It definitely started before high school. They were always really nice about it! They would tell my parents that I was bright and had a lot of potential... but they did mention, um, that I rushed through things sometimes. And that I maybe talked out of turn a little bit. 

I was just so eager to participate, I think! But I guess I did forget my homework sometimes, too. Oh, and my desk... it was always a bit of a mess, just stuffed with half-finished papers and drawings. I always meant to finish them, I really did! I just got excited about the next thing, I suppose. 

Does that sound like the kind of thing you're looking for? I hope I wasn't a bad kid or anything... I think a lot of kids are a little disorganized at that age, right?

Um, at home? Well, honestly, I think I'm okay with chores! I mean, I like having a tidy space, so I try really hard to keep things nice. 

But... I guess where I do struggle with organizing things—like you mentioned with work—is, well, work itself. Um, when I'm trying to pull together a campaign, I... I kind of end up with my notes everywhere. Like, some will be in my emails, some on sticky notes, some in my planner... and then I'll have all these random tabs open on my computer. 

I really do try to stay on top of it, but sometimes I end up wasting so much time just trying to figure out what I'm supposed to do first, instead of actually starting the work. I know that probably sounds a bit messy, and I'm really working on finding a better system! I'm sure everyone gets a little overwhelmed when they have a lot of different pieces to manage, right? Is it okay that I brought it back to work instead of home? I just wanted to be honest about where I actually notice it happening.

Is that the kind of thing you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that you've been managing these things okay, but have these patterns ever caused you to make significant mistakes, miss important deadlines, or receive formal feedback from your supervisors about your performance?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well... I guess I missed a few details in a pitch because I got a little sidetracked, and they did complain to my manager. It was just a miscommunication, really! I've been trying extra hard since then to double-check everything. \n\nI really hope my boss doesn't think I'm a bad employee. I put in a lot of extra hours to make up for it! Does that sound like a normal thing to happen when you're busy, or is that the kind of mistake you're asking about?\n\nDoctor: Aside from your work environment, do you notice these same patterns of getting distracted or feeling the need to move around when you are at home or in other social settings?\n\nPatient: Um, gosh, at home or with friends? I... I don't really think so. Or at least, I haven't really noticed it being an issue. \n\nI mean, I really try to be present when I'm with people.
